## DeepSeek seats via OpenRig `omp` (2026-10-04, verified live)

Owner asked for a DeepSeek seat for the supervisor loop so the loop costs no Claude window. Built, running, and it has completed a tick.

- **There is no `deepseek` runtime.** OpenRig's supported set is `claude-code`, `codex`, `pi`, `omp`, `agy`, `terminal`, `stub` (`packages/daemon/src/domain/rigspec-preflight.ts:147`). DeepSeek is a *provider*, and `omp` is the runtime that carries it: `pi-runner-protocol.ts:132` maps `deepseek` -> `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY`, and a seat receives a provider key only when its `model` is written `provider/id` (`docs/reference/rig-spec.md:295`).
- **Verified live:** `omp` v18.6.0 at `~/.local/bin/omp`, installed with `install.sh --binary` (the default bun path fails: local bun 1.2.3 < required 1.3.14). `omp --model deepseek/deepseek-flash -p --no-tools` returns a correct answer. Model ids from `GET https://api.deepseek.com/models`: `deepseek-flash`, `deepseek-v4-pro`.
- **Rig:** `4genthub-deepseek` (`01M431AAP55S5KEAPB56QP0K2E`), one seat `supervisor`, `runtime: omp`, `model: deepseek/deepseek-flash`, `permission_policy: builtin:yolo`. Spec at `~/.openrig/agenthub-seats/4genthub-deepseek/`. `rig spec validate` valid and `rig up --plan` preflight ok before launch; the seat then booted, received its guidance and ran.
- **`builtin:yolo` is required, not cosmetic:** it resolves to `launch_posture=full_bypass` (`policy-ref.ts:186`), which the OMP adapter turns into `--approval-mode yolo` (`pi-runtime-adapter.ts:244`). omp's default floor is `always-ask`, and headless those approvals are cancelled, parking the seat in needing-attention. Launch reports this as a warning by design.
- **Credential provisioning:** `recovery.provider_auth_env_allowlist` is daemon-global user settings, and it forwards only vars already present in the daemon's environment — daemon pid 17485 has no `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY`, so that path needs a daemon restart (12 live seats across 2 rigs). Instead: omp reads the launch directory's `.env`, so the rig root holds `.env` -> symlink to `~/.config/deepseek/env`. Verified with `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY` removed from the environment: it still authenticated. No secret is copied.
- **Cloud gap — CLOSED 2026-10-04.** The Go runtime list was `claude-code`, `codex`, `agy` (`fastmcp/seat_management/domain/resolver/runtime.go`, the one list read by API validation, rigspec, the seed library and the renderer), so the cloud could not store or render this seat. `RuntimeOmp` is now in that list; `receivesClaudeFragments` was narrowed from "not codex and not agy" to `== claude-code` so a runtime added later defaults to receiving no Claude fragment instead of silently getting one; the MCP `manage_seat` text names all four runtimes; and the tests that asserted `omp` was invalid were corrected to assert the new truth. Evidence: the `omp` subtest of `TestRenderSeatRigValidate` passes (the rendered spec goes through the real `rig agent validate`), `go vet ./...` is clean, and `go test ./...` is green. `pi` remains unsupported by design.
- **First tick, reported by the seat itself (2026-10-04):** the loop ran and reported — the Claude window is capped and `agy` is out of service, so both pools were down (zero dev capacity at that moment); it parked for 3600 s rather than stopping. Owner-stated 2026-10-04: the agy pool has hit its limit, about 167 hours, so the supervisor is told to skip it entirely — no probe, no nudge — until that reset passes. It also flagged that the team labels in its brief (and in Request 18's wording here) are stale: both `4genthub-min` and `4genthub-dev` render a Claude statusline and both are capped, which matches Request 16's note that `4genthub-dev` moved back to `claude-code` on 2026-10-03 after the Antigravity quota ran out. Owner decision needed: treat both rigs as one Claude pool? It also prompted a check that changed the guidance.
- **`4genthub-dev` is a Claude pool, and OpenRig's own registry says otherwise** (verified 2026-10-04). `rig usage series --lane provider_window --since <6h ago> --json` returned 588 samples, emitted by all nine `4genthub-dev` seats (web-dev 108, lead 88, go-dev 82, tester 82, reviewer 70, writer 32, debugger 26, planner 8, architect 6) and none by `4genthub-min`. That lane is written only from Claude Code's statusline JSON (`packages/daemon/src/domain/usage-samples-store.ts:115`, source `claude_statusline_json`, in the OpenRig checkout), so those nine are real Claude-subscription seats. Meanwhile `rig ps --nodes --rig 4genthub-dev --json` reports `runtime: agy` for all nine, and the rig's `rig.yaml` declares `agy` too. The registry is stale; the telemetry is not. Consequence: a "no cap" rule aimed at `4genthub-dev` would have let nine Claude seats run the window to zero. Related: a 10-minute usage window came back empty while the 6-hour window held 588 samples, so short windows mislead.
- **Supervisor guidance updated accordingly** (2026-10-04): the deployed `role.md` in `~/.openrig/agenthub-seats/4genthub-deepseek/agents/supervisor/guidance/` now classifies each pool from evidence rather than rig name — `provider_window` samples are the authoritative signal (a session that appears there is a Claude seat), the `rig ps` `runtime` field is advisory only and explicitly distrusted, and a disagreement caps the pool and is reported as an owner decision rather than resolved silently. The seat was relaunched fresh (generation `a432acfa-b2ad-4c6d-a69b-fa36235baa36`) and confirmed running the new text.
- **A usage percentage is only meaningful for the seats you are gating** (verified 2026-10-04 by the seat). Over the same period, a herdr session's `provider_window` rows read `five_hour = 2%` while the nine `4genthub-dev` seats read 94-98%. The lane answers "which seats are on Claude" globally, but the number belongs to the seat that emitted it, so gating on another session's figure shows headroom the gated seats do not have. The guidance now says to read that rig's own seats. Whether the difference is separate credentials or separate windows was not determined.
- **The seat's delegated review worker failed where the principal session's succeeded** (2026-10-04): `omp --model deepseek/deepseek-flash -p --no-tools` run from inside the seat returned unusable tool-call markup instead of a review, twice. The same command from a principal Claude Code session, where `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY` is in the environment, produced an accurate adversarial review that found four real defects in `2c8d05f3`. The guidance now tells the seat not to retry in a loop, to review the diff itself, and to record the failed path.
- **Correction to the line above (supervisor tick, 2026-10-04 11:04Z): the delegated worker does work from inside the seat.** `omp --model deepseek/deepseek-flash -p --no-tools` with the diff attached via `@file` and a prompt that states the worker has no tools or filesystem access and must not emit tool-call markup returned a usable review in 57 s — it named its own scope limit ("I cannot verify from the diff alone that every other client-side runtime enumeration was caught") instead of inventing findings. Patch: `f66d25a4` + `4376846a`, 117 lines, already reviewed, so this tests the invocation, not the finding power. The two failures were the bare prompt over a 621-line and a 1147-line patch, so the undetermined variable is prompt wording versus diff size; keep the patch small and state the no-tools constraint until that is pinned.
- **The seat found a real gap in the omp change**: the "no `runtime/` file" loop in `renderer_test.go` covered `codex` and `agy` only, so the narrowed `receivesClaudeFragments` for `omp` was unasserted. Fixed and proven by mutation. Its own per-seat usage read (94-98%) was also more accurate than the global read (2%) this session took from a non-seat sample.

- `4bc935a3` (2026-10-05, reviewed) **the seat-model deletion gap.** `DELETE /api/v2/openrig/rooms/{room}` is MOUNTED (`seat_admin_mount.go:285`) and previously CASCADED: `RoomDeletionService.DeleteRoom` listed the room's seats inside the transaction and hard-deleted each seat's links, overlay, resolved snapshot, status and row before deleting the room. It now REFUSES a non-empty room with the new sentinel `ErrRoomNotEmpty`, naming the count (`room not empty: room "dev" still holds 2 seat(s); remove them first`), mapped to **409 Conflict** beside the other conflicts, the count taken from the same `ListSeats` read the guard decided on; an EMPTY room still hard-deletes its overlay, reported statuses and row in one transaction. Shape unchanged: hard delete, application-layer cascade, no FK cascade; seats are emptied through the existing per-seat `DELETE`, untouched. A test asserts every recorded store call is tx-begin or tx-end, so on refusal nothing was TOUCHED rather than merely surviving. **The link delete was ALREADY CORRECT** and the commit carries no code change for it: `DELETE .../seats/{seat}/links/{to}/{kind}` hard-deletes the row enforcement reads — the resolver's policy snapshot reads `seat_links` live (`seat_resolution_service.go:232-262`) and the rigspec renderer reads them live (`seat_rigspec_mount.go:183-223`) — and a resolver-level test now proves the link is gone from the ENFORCING SET by consuming it before the delete and not after, rather than by asserting a list length. Only the mounts could say which half was a behaviour fix and which a proof of existing behaviour. `agenthub-frontend/src/docs/api-reference.en.md` now reads "Delete an empty room; refused (409) while it still holds seats."
- `bf0a3ded` (2026-10-05, reviewed) **the seat-creation runtime fallback.** A seat created WITHOUT a runtime now takes the resolved seat-type version's `default_runtime` and is validated THERE; an explicit runtime still wins and is still validated first; an empty default still 400s with no invented default; and the model is still validated after the fallback. The precedence change on the omitted path — an unknown room is now reported before the runtime — is INTENDED and stated in the code comment, because the version is not resolved yet at that point, so there is nothing to validate the runtime against.
- `c9483b6c` (2026-10-06, reviewed) **the composer invariant asserted.** `ComposeProfile` (`fastmcp/seat_management/domain/contextpacks/compose.go`) replaced the `byID` fallback with an internal-invariant error (`internal invariant violated: atom %q was queued for closure but is not selected`), which is unreachable BY CONSTRUCTION — both queue-entry points set `selected` in the same iteration, `selected` is never deleted from, and the self-dependency case continues at `:189` — so the old fallback would have closed over the zero atom and silently thinned the walk. The report sentence was corrected to match the line. (This is the Go `seat_management/domain/contextpacks` composer, distinct from the frontend `domain/context-packs/profile-composer.ts` described under F2.)

- **The token contract, stated in one sentence (2026-10-06, the reviewer's measurement).** The minting path DECLARES each token class in `fastmcp/auth/domain/services/jwt_service.go`: `:192` session tokens carry `"type","access"` AND `email`; `:219` `"refresh"`; `:231` `"reset"`; `:244` API tokens carry `"type","api_token"` with SCOPES and NO `email`. The claim is already READ in at least four places — `jwt_service.go:459` validates on it, `unified_token_validator.go:57` reads it, `dual_auth_middleware.go:196` branches on `type == "api_token"`, and `auth_root_test.go:166` pins the minted value. **So the defect was never a MISSING contract: it is a contract nobody had stated in one sentence, and the app-side fix `8894ea1b` reads as A READER THAT DID NOT KNOW THE RULE rather than a mint that omitted one.** IN FLIGHT, not done: the app-side refinement (web-dev) classifies on the DECLARED TYPE before the email check, so an `api_token` is refused because IT SAYS SO rather than because it lacks a field; and the backend row (go-dev) is now ACTIVE — `b6d5db94` fired its trigger — as DOCUMENT THE EXISTING CLAIM PLUS PIN BOTH SHAPES — go-dev's own reasoning, worth carrying: a comment alone is documentation that cannot fail, so the TEST is what makes the contract checkable. **CLOSED 2026-10-06 — THE PREMISE IS STALE BECAUSE THE DIVERGENCE WAS ALREADY REMOVED (verified by go-dev; recorded as a closure rather than as a row closure because it NEVER WAS A QUEUE ROW — the board carried it as a backlog line and the lead sent a seat after a row that does not exist).** `facade_wiring.go:78` is now a **COMMENT carrying the removal and its reason**: the factory used to invent `JWT_SECRET_KEY=default-jwt-secret-key-for-token-facade-32b` when the variable was empty, which made the secret **PROCESS-LOCAL state** — the minting process accepted its own tokens while every replica that never minted refused them (**the same token returned 101 from one and 403 from the other two**) — and `auth/providers/jwt_bearer.go:51-54` refuses an unset variable with `missingSecretError`, whose text at **`:73`** names the variable (`JWT_SECRET_KEY must be provided or set in environment`). **So BOTH PATHS NOW REFUSE AN UNSET SECRET — one rule, unset means refuse** — the fixes being `8a6976bf` (the default removed) and `a53453ec` (the mint path naming the missing secret in the same sentence the REST path uses). **THE DIVERGENCE WAS DEMONSTRATED LIVE BEFORE THE FIX:** a two-process run with the variable unset returned **101 on the minting process and 403 on the other two**, with the token row active, unexpired and hash-matching — so **scope was not the discriminator, the secret was**. **AND THE RESTART RULE IS DOCUMENTED WHERE AN OPERATOR MEETS IT:** a token is valid only where the **same** secret is configured, which is a **DEPLOYMENT property** because the token row stores no secret — `.env.sample:147` carries it (`Must be set (>=32 chars) and identical on every process and every restart`) with `:150` adding `do not reuse a minted token across a restart (or a deploy) that changes it`. **THE DECISION DIRECTION IS RECORDED WITH ITS REASON, because go-dev asked whether to re-open it and the answer was NO:** defaulting the **VALIDATORS** was rejected because it would have spread a **PUBLIC CONSTANT into auth verification on every path** — the same defect as the hardcoded default, only wider — so the choice turned on **a property of the failure** rather than on preference. **AND THE ITEM WAS REAL WITHOUT BEING OWNED, the SAME GAP the owner found in the demands:** it existed in `NEXT_GEN.md` as prose with no queue row, and a board passed it on as though a row existed — **a backlog line is not an owner.**

**Corrected fact — the `secretscan` package is IN the tree and committed (2026-10-06).** An earlier message reported that `.gitignore:67` (`*secret*`) hides the whole `secretscan/` directory, so the package and its testdata would never be committed. Measured here, that is wrong: `git ls-files` lists THREE tracked files under `agenthub_go/fastmcp/seat_management/domain/secretscan/` (`secretscan.go`, `secretscan_test.go`, `testdata/scan_cases.json`), and `git check-ignore -v` on the tracked path matches NO rule (rc=1 — tracked paths are not ignored). The stronger fact, which stops a reader worrying about the next file: `.gitignore:71` is a DELIBERATE NEGATION, `!agenthub_go/fastmcp/seat_management/domain/secretscan/**`, so even a NEW file in that directory is not ignored (`git check-ignore --no-index` proves it on a probe path AND on the real `testdata/scan_cases.json`). So `*secret*` at `:67` is real and the scanner directory is explicitly kept out of its reach. The load-bearing eight-pattern scanner cited all night is therefore the committed package that refuses credential-shaped values at module load and at render — nobody needs to re-derive it, and nobody should treat it as untracked. **AND DO NOT REMOVE LINES 70-71.** This correction is unusual, and the reason is worth stating: a stale claim is usually just stale, and this one invites a REPAIR THAT WOULD DO HARM. The root `.gitignore` carries TWO EXPLICIT NEGATIONS — `:70` `!agenthub_go/fastmcp/seat_management/domain/secretscan/` and `:71` the same path with `/**` — so `*secret*` at `:67` fails to reach the package ONLY because a later negation RE-INCLUDES IT DELIBERATELY, with the path spelled out twice. A POSITIVE CONTROL confirms the rule is live rather than dead: `git check-ignore --no-index -v foo_secret_bar.txt` still reports `.gitignore:67:*secret*`. So anyone who reads the stale log and "fixes the pattern" would DELETE AN INTENTIONAL UN-IGNORE and UN-TRACK a load-bearing package. The correction is therefore not complete until the wrong action it suggests is named as wrong — the same family as rule 10 and rule 22's recovery: name the repair that would do harm, not only the fact that was wrong.

**Cleanup list, measured — the `gofmt` finding is not on this tree (2026-10-06).** The mission's item 4 names TWO cleanups, the 23 TypeScript errors and a "gofmt finding in the subtask controller"; its first clause — remaining Go port items in `NEXT_GEN.md` — is a CATEGORY, not a third cleanup (an earlier reading of it as "two of the three" was corrected). Measured with the tool itself, the second cleanup does not exist: `gofmt -l` over every tracked `.go` file in the module (`git ls-files '*.go'`) prints ZERO, `gofmt -l cmd fastmcp` prints ZERO, `gofmt -l fastmcp/task_management/interface` prints ZERO, and `gofmt -d` on the named controller (`fastmcp/task_management/interface/mcp_controllers/subtask_mcp_controller/subtask_mcp_controller.go`) has NO diff — the strongest form of clean, with the empty diff stated as the other direction so a reader sees it was checked and not merely unmentioned. Both named cleanups are therefore closed by measurement (the 23 TypeScript errors measured clean earlier), and the only open part of item 4 is the **Go-port-items category**, whose members the 2026-10-05 reconciliation already listed — so if the owner asks what remains in that item, the honest answer is: both named cleanups closed by measurement, and the Go-port items as the open category. (`gofmt -l .` from `agenthub_go/` does print files — all under `.gomodcache/` and `.gotmp/`; those are caches and build temp, not this module's source, which is why the scoped command is the one quoted. That unscoped run is a check that REPORTS A PROBLEM WHERE NONE EXISTS — rule 16's family, opposite direction.) **AND THE FINDING'S OWN LINE, MARKED CLOSED WITH ITS FIX RATHER THAN AS AN ABSENCE (2026-10-06; go-dev's words, verified here): "subtask controller gofmt — CLOSED by `a4449d25` (2026-10-03), verified clean at HEAD by tree-wide gofmt and by before/after extraction".** **WHAT MAKES THAT A CLOSURE RATHER THAN AN ABSENCE, which is the part this entry was missing: the tree-wide formatter reports NOTHING for the whole Go tree, AND THE FIX IS IDENTIFIED — `a4449d25` IS AN ANCESTOR OF HEAD, 488 commits back (verified here with `git rev-list --count`), and EXTRACTING THE FILE AT ITS PARENT AND RUNNING THE FORMATTER PRINTS THE PATH while the same file at HEAD prints nothing — so THE FINDING WAS REAL AND THAT COMMIT IS WHAT CLOSED IT.** **The line STAYS rather than being deleted, so a future reader does not re-chase it.** **AND THE SECOND HALF, which is a rule APPLIED rather than stated: NO CHANGELOG LINE AND NO COMMIT were produced for this closure, because a changelog entry for a fix that was ALREADY MADE and ALREADY RECORDED would be THE SAME FACT TWICE — WHEN A CHECKLIST ITEM TURNS OUT TO BE ALREADY DONE, THE DELIVERABLE IS THE MEASUREMENT AND NOT A DUPLICATE ENTRY.**

## Process lessons

**Rules this session had to discover (2026-10-05), each with the incident that produced it:**

1. CITE BY ANCESTRY, NOT BY CONTENT. `git merge-base --is-ancestor <hash> <tip>` (or `git branch --contains`) is the membership test; an empty `git diff --stat` between two hashes is not, because an amended-away commit stays content-identical and resolvable while being in no branch. Incident: an amend left an orphaned duplicate (`032e69c0` vs `daa7d22a`, same subject — checked with `git merge-base --is-ancestor`: the first is NOT an ancestor of main and the second is) that was named to two seats before the reviewer checked membership.
2. NAME THE ROOT YOU MEASURED, IN THE CLAIM ITSELF. A number measured on a working tree is not a number about a commit, and a number measured in a copy is not a number about the repo. Incident: three separate cases — a grep over the working tree labelled HEAD; tree-level frontend greens read as commit-level; and a delegated worker reporting success against `/tmp/fe-merge`. The fix was a provenance label, not a re-measurement. **AND THE FAMILY'S THIRD INSTANCE, WHICH EXTENDS IT FROM WHAT a number is ABOUT to WHAT WAS ACTUALLY RUNNING (2026-10-06; found by the seat that was about to make the mistake): A RUN AGAINST A STALE BINARY ANSWERS ABOUT THE OLD CODE.** **The catch: the probe binary about to be tested PREDATED THAT SEAT'S OWN EDITS BY ELEVEN HOURS, so it REBUILT BEFORE RUNNING.** **The three members, recorded together because they are one family: A LANDED FIX IS INVISIBLE UNTIL THE REBUILT BINARY RESTARTS; A COMMITTED TIP IS READ AS THE WORKING TREE; and now A STALE ARTIFACT IS TRUSTED AS THE BUILD.** **THE OPERATIVE SENTENCE: BEFORE BELIEVING ANY RUN, KNOW WHAT WAS ACTUALLY RUNNING — AND A BINARY'S TIMESTAMP IS PART OF THE EVIDENCE RATHER THAN A DETAIL.**
3. A COUNT OF TEXT IS NOT A COUNT OF STRUCTURE, and a discrepancy between two counts of one thing is evidence to CHECK rather than a story to tell. Incident, re-derivable — and the numbers MOVE WITH THE TREE, which is why the rule is the anchoring and not the count: on `fastmcp/seat_management/infrastructure/schema/seat_management_postgresql.sql`, `grep -c 'CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS'` vs `grep -cE '^CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS'` gave 14 vs 13 when the incident happened and gives 16 vs 15 on the tree after D5 appended TEAMS; in both states the gap is the header COMMENT at line 6, so the plausible mechanism ("a table created twice") was wrong.
4. AGREEMENT BETWEEN TWO INSTRUMENTS WRITTEN THE SAME WAY IS NOT INDEPENDENT CONFIRMATION. Incident, recorded durably in the surface inventory (`ai_docs/api-integration/surface-inventory.md`, section 5 "Contradictions between existing docs and the code", bullet 5 "Cross-check `/tmp/inv.md`", line 554): a mount inventory built from `HandleFunc` lines and a grep written the same way shared one blind spot — mounts made by CONTROLLER METHOD CALL (`app.go:119-120`, `NewAuthController().RegisterRoutes(mux)`) — so both reported 112 httpapp registrations and neither saw the 20 auth ones, and their agreement produced a false "undocumented split" report to the owner. The reference has since been regenerated (24 auth mentions where it had none) and httpapp is 120 after D5; the counts move, the shared blind spot is the lesson.
5. NEVER REWRITE A HASH SOMEONE HAS READ. Correct a message with a NEW commit that says what it corrects; the wrong sentence is instructive once the correction is visible beside it. Incident: the same amend that rules 1's orphan records — the commit had already been named to two seats when it was rewritten, so the correction had to arrive as a new commit, and the orphaned original (`032e69c0`) is only detectable by ancestry. Tonight's own corrections follow the rule: the tick's two follow-ups are later commits rather than an amend. **AND THE BLUNTER RULING THAT SUPERSEDES THE CHECK (2026-10-06, from THE FIRST AMEND IN THIS TREE TO LAND ON ANOTHER SEAT'S COMMIT — the ordinal was first written as "a second amend", which was TRUE OF NOTHING: tonight had exactly one amend, and the count was miscarried once by a grep that matched the reporting commit's own subject because the subject contains the word — verified at the history by the lead rather than taken from either seat's account, then re-measured here): DO NOT AMEND IN THIS TREE.** **The corrective check is real — PROVE THAT HEAD IS STILL YOUR COMMIT, AFTER the add rather than before it — BUT IT IS A CHECK FOUR SEATS HAVE TO PERFORM PERFECTLY EVERY TIME, AND THE COST OF GETTING IT WRONG IS REWRITING SOMEBODY ELSE'S HISTORY RATHER THAN ADDING A STRAY COMMIT OF YOUR OWN.** **AND THE AVAILABLE ALTERNATIVE IS BORING AND ALWAYS SAFE: IF A GATE FAILED AFTER YOU COMMITTED, COMMIT THE FIX ON TOP.** **THE RULE IS NOT "AMENDING IS BAD" — AMENDING IS NOT A NEW PRACTICE HERE, and the reflog holds SIX DISTINCT REWRITTEN HASHES ACROSS THE PREVIOUS THREE DAYS (`git reflog --all --date=short | grep 'commit (amend)'`: fourteen lines, the object being DISTINCT HASHES because each operation appears once for HEAD and once for the branch) EVERY ONE OF WHICH REWROTE ITS OWN AUTHOR'S COMMIT (one corrects a justification, one ticks a checklist, one removes a debug helper) — IT IS "AMENDING IS SAFE ONLY WHILE NOBODY ELSE COMMITS", WHICH IS A CONDITION NOBODY CAN CHECK RELIABLY IN A TREE WITH FOUR SEATS AND THEREFORE NOT A CONDITION TO RELY ON.** **THE PRACTICE WAS SAFE SIX TIMES AND FAILED THE FIRST TIME A SECOND SEAT COMMITTED BETWEEN THE ADD AND THE AMEND.** **The incident, as measured rather than as told: A SEAT'S OWN COMMIT WAS TWO REFLOG ENTRIES BACK WHEN IT RAN THE AMEND, HEAD HAD MOVED TO THIS SEAT'S COMMIT IN BETWEEN, and THE AMEND REWROTE THIS SEAT'S COMMIT INTO A NEW HASH WITH A FORMATTING LINE INSIDE IT.** **The repair was a SOFT RESET back to the original hash followed by a fresh commit on top, and the lead CONFIRMED IT INDEPENDENTLY FROM THE HISTORY: THE ORIGINAL COMMIT IS PRESENT WITH ITS OWN STAT (two files, three insertions, one deletion); THE AMENDED COMMIT STILL EXISTS AS AN OBJECT WITH NO REF POINTING AT IT, so nothing reaches it; THE REFLOG CARRIES THE SEQUENCE IN THAT ORDER; and THE CULPRIT SEAT'S OWN EARLIER COMMIT IS UNTOUCHED BENEATH IT — SO THE PUBLISHED HASH IS STILL VALID AND EVERY REFERENCE TO IT STILL POINTS AT THE RIGHT BYTES.** **AND TWO SUPPORTING FACTS, BOTH WORTH THEIR LINE: (i) THE CAUSE WAS A GATE THAT PRINTED A FAILURE AND WAS NOT TREATED AS FAILED — the formatter NAMED the file, the name was read, and the commit went through anyway, which is A DIFFERENT FAILURE FROM THE NIGHT'S OTHER INSTRUMENT INSTANCES because THERE THE TOOL SPOKE AND THE OPERATOR PROCEEDED REGARDLESS; and (ii) THE NON-EVENT, VERIFIED IN THE SAME READ AND SO RECORDED AS CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED — THE COMMIT SWEPT NOBODY'S UNCOMMITTED WORK, its stat being two files BEFORE and AFTER the amend.**
6. A SEAT'S VERDICTS BELONG TO THE SEAT, NOT THE OCCUPANT. Rows carry the transitions, so attribute to the seat, and name a specific verdict when it needs today's measurement rather than inheriting it. Incident, re-measured with BOTH frames named because this rule is about a seat's own verdicts and the direction changes the number: from the REVIEWER's frame — the frame this rule is about — `rig queue list --destination 4genthub-min-reviewer@4genthub-min --all` in the 16:xx hour gives SIX rows destined to the reviewer (16:10:33 to 16:32:20) — four sourced from this seat's hand-offs (16:10:33, 16:17:15, 16:19:14, 16:32:20) and two from fe-dev (16:14:49, 16:21:54); the prior-occupant boundary group is the 16:17–16:32 four (16:17:15, 16:19:14, 16:21:54, 16:32:20), three of them this seat's and one fe-dev's; from THIS seat's frame the same command gives SEVEN (16:08:56 to 16:55:50). Both are true of one side of the queue relation, and the reviewer's own first row is 17:39:49 — either way the seat's record predates any occupant's memory, so "the seat said so" is only usable while the rows can be read, and the direction has to be named with the number.
7. WHEN A CLAIM SAYS A THING EXISTS AT HEAD, EVALUATE IT AGAINST HEAD. Where peers keep untracked in-flight files, that distinction is load-bearing rather than pedantic. Incident: a board tick cited `SessionList.tsx:33` as HEAD evidence and the file is not tracked at all.
8. A GUARD THAT VALIDATES ONE WRITE IS BLIND TO A LATER WRITE TO A DIFFERENT STORE; AN INVARIANT SPANNING TWO WRITES NEEDS AN OWNER AND A NAME. Two writes that must agree, each validated alone, can still disagree, and nothing reports it until the invariant is named. Incidents: the pin/overlay pair (a `pin` at one scope and a `remove` at another are each valid yet jointly violate what a pin reads as); the never-assigned seam; the two-literals-one-concept (one server described two ways).
9. AN INSTRUMENT THAT SHARES A SOURCE WITH THE THING IT VERIFIES CANNOT FAIL. When the check and the checked read the same source, agreement is guaranteed and proves nothing. Incidents: the rollback leg reading the returned-backup text; the drift check reading its own source; the resolved hash witnessing a version move when the question is a follow-latest toggle. Fix: an independent source, or state plainly that the check is corroboration rather than confirmation.
10. BRIEF FROM THE ROW'S LIVE STATE, NOT FROM A PLAN TABLE; CHECK THE ROW IS NOT ALREADY TERMINAL BEFORE WRITING THE BRIEF. A plan table is a pointer to work, not a ledger of it — the ledger is the queue, and rows go terminal while a table still reads as current. Incident: two briefs in ten minutes were for already-delivered work — web-dev was assigned F6 (which is `630b2bd0` with the live half `419d57d8`) and D1/D2/D4 frontend (whose palette is `88fe3852` with the fix in `019dff6d`) — because the D1-D4 slice plan table in this file was read as state.
11. A PATTERN'S BOUNDARY BELONGS INSIDE THE CLAIM: "nothing matches `<pattern>`" asserts there is no match for THAT pattern — it does NOT assert "nothing asserts this". State the pattern with the claim, and widen it (case, anchoring) to the sentence around it. **Third instance tonight of the same act — extending a check past what it measured.** Incident: `57de3715` claimed no determinism test existed; the pattern was case-sensitive for a capitalised name while `TestResolveHashDeterministic` already sat at `resolver_test.go:344`. **A CLAUSE ON THIS RULE, the SHARPEST member of the family (2026-10-06, the reviewer's own retraction): BEFORE REPORTING AN ABSENCE, MAKE THE INSTRUMENT ABLE TO SEE THE PRESENCE.** It grepped for lowercase `reads back` and reported the item MISSING while the line reads `READS BACK INCLUDING mcp` — **a case-sensitive pattern reporting an absence it could not have found**. **An absence claim from an instrument that cannot match is UNFALSIFIABLE BY THE PERSON MAKING IT**, which is what separates it from a wrong value: a wrong value can be caught by its own author, an absence cannot. So the fix is always to **WIDEN THE INSTRUMENT BEFORE WIDENING THE CLAIM** — the fifth instance of the instrument class tonight, and the FIRST that is about absence rather than about a value. **AND THE CLAUSE WAS LOAD-BEARING WITHIN THE HOUR, USED BY A SEAT THAT DID NOT WRITE IT:** this seat's own absence checks in the stale-claim pass were run **CASE-INSENSITIVELY because of this clause**, which is why the `call_agent` search returned the surviving FIELD (on `manage_agent`) instead of reporting the string absent — a case-sensitive search would have produced the opposite and equally wrong statement. Written, then used, then cited: the same load-bearing test the rules reconciliation applied to 18/30/38/40.
12. WHEN A GUARD CANNOT BE A BOUNDARY, ITS ACCEPTANCE HAS TO BE ABOUT WHAT IT RECORDS, NOT WHAT IT STOPS. G3 is L2 by design — the sender identity header is forgeable, a shell wrapper can step around a Claude deny prefix, and `audit-scan` is the net — so the seatcheck acceptance is REFUSED / DETECTED / NEITHER per case, not blocked-or-not. A guard described as "blocks X" is tested by whether X happened; the same guard described as "flags X" is tested by whether the flag exists — a claim the mechanism can keep, whereas one stronger than its mechanism is how the `/health` seam, the unwired `MissedStore` and tonight's naming variants stayed invisible.
13. THE SILENT-FAILURE CLASS: THE FAILURE IS NOT THAT THE WRONG THING HAPPENS, IT IS THAT NOTHING HAPPENS — no error, no row, no flag — and the system's own evidence is silent about the case it exists to catch. Incidents: Q1 in the pin design (an unpin that resolves identically is invisible in the resolved hash); the never-assigned health seam; and the invisible bypass, which go-dev sharpened from "a forged send must be blocked" to "an invisible bypass must not exist", because the second is fixable and testable offline from the pinned snapshot.

14. THE RECIPE HAD NEVER BEEN RUN END TO END BEFORE TONIGHT — A PLAN DOCUMENT NOTHING HAS EXECUTED IS NOT A VERIFIED INSTRUCTION. (Rule 10 by one layer) A recipe, a plan table and a mission file are pointers to work, and until something follows them they are unexecuted prose. Incident: the local-stack recipe, followed verbatim for the first time on 2026-10-05/06, produced FIVE omissions — four of them in the first hour — (the `DATABASE_*` set; the `Authorization: Bearer` rule; `AGENTHUB_PUBLIC_URL` on the seat GET; the REST-versus-WebSocket token split; and the skill-catalog seeding ORDER) — the stack worked, the instructions did not.
15. A NUMBER TRAVELS WITH THE PATTERN, THE FILE SET, AND WHETHER IT COUNTS LINES OR OCCURRENCES. (Rule 3, extended) **AND THE OBJECT CLAUSE (added 2026-10-06, because the same rule had to be spelled out TWICE IN ONE PACKET — which is itself the signal that it belongs in the standard the seats read rather than only in an entry): A NUMBER IS ONLY MEANINGFUL WITH THE OBJECT IT COUNTS.** **The two instances, both from one shift: TWENTY-ONE against THIRTY-NINE (occurrences in GO COMMENTS against occurrences in DOCUMENTATION PROSE — both right about their own object, and ONLY ONE of them describing the commit) and 741 against 140 and 138 (test CASES INCLUDING SUBTESTS; TOP-LEVEL SKIP LINES; and the TOP-LEVEL CASE-COUNT difference).** **So NAME THE OBJECT IN THE SAME SENTENCE as the number: the pattern and the file set are not enough, because two counts of the same thing can differ by the LAYER THEY WALK.** **AND ITS FOURTH INSTANCE INTRODUCES A NEW UNIT — A CLAIM AND AN INSTANCE ARE DIFFERENT UNITS (2026-10-06): THE BATCH COUNTED CLAIM INSTANCES WHILE THE COMMIT EDITED CLAIMS BY BLOCK, so THREE SAME-FILE SECOND INSTANCES REMAINED with both the count and the edit looking complete — A COUNT AND AN EDIT THAT USE DIFFERENT UNITS LOOK LIKE A MISSING EDIT.** **The corrected form, recorded because it is the shape to copy: FIFTEEN CLAIM INSTANCES AUDITED, FOURTEEN FALSE, ONE TRUE; OF THE FOURTEEN FALSE, THIRTEEN CORRECTED AND THREE REMAINING — WHERE REMAINING EXCEEDS UNCORRECTED BECAUSE THREE OF THE FOURTEEN WERE MULTI-INSTANCE CLAIMS.** **AND NOTE WHERE IT FIRED: this is the FIRST time the discrepancy appeared WITHIN ONE SEAT'S OWN WORK rather than BETWEEN TWO SEATS — which says the rule is about COUNTING rather than about DISAGREEMENT, and is why it lives here rather than in the reconciliation rules.** Incident, three shapes of one thing: the registration count read 120, 121 and 122 (working tree versus HEAD; `_test.go` included or not; which `mux.HandleFunc` form); tonight's seatcheck run read **205** (new file plus its directory) and **212** (the whole directory) — both true; and beside them the digest count, where the pinned file's canonical **51** and plugin **41** count digest ENTRIES, not skills, while the inventory's **52** is a union of 35 + 19 − 2. State the pattern, the file set and the unit, or the number is not a fact. **AND THE MECHANISM FROM THE ARITHMETIC SIDE (2026-10-06; go-dev's, with BOTH instances from his own day): A STALE ARITHMETIC AND A DOUBLE-APPLIED FILTER ARE BOTH FAILURES OF DESCRIBING THE OPERANDS** — the first computes against a **tree that has moved**, the second applies a **filter the operand already carries** — **AND IN BOTH CASES THE RESULT LOOKS LIKE A MEASUREMENT, BECAUSE IT IS A NUMBER PRODUCED BY A DEFINED OPERATION.** **The asymmetry is the one this file keeps meeting: THE OPERATION WAS PERFORMED CORRECTLY AND THE OPERAND WAS DESCRIBED WRONGLY** — a true sentence attached to the wrong subject — **and the repair is the same: SAY WHAT THE NUMBER IS A COUNT OF, INCLUDING ANY FILTERS ALREADY INSIDE IT.** The instances: a figure **computed before a deletion and quoted after it**, and **a subtraction that removed a filter the figure already carried** — and go-dev **WITHDREW the resulting `1,179` rather than leaving a fourth number in a row about counts**, which is exactly the discipline this rule asks for. **AND THE OPERAND QUESTION IN A DIFFERENT UNIT, one line: TWO LINES MATCHING A STRING ARE NOT TWO FILES MATCHING A PATTERN** — a grep counts **LINES** containing a substring, and whether those are the files you meant is **one read away**, which is why it stays an **UNREAD DETAIL rather than a claim in a row about exact counts.** **AND THE INVERSE ERROR, ADDED THE SAME DAY FROM FOUR INSTANCES — AN OBJECT CHOSEN TO FIT A NUMBER: A COUNT WITHOUT ITS OBJECT UNDERSTATES WHAT EXISTS, WHILE AN OBJECT CHOSEN FOR A NUMBER INVENTS WHAT DOES NOT.** **The two look identical from outside, because both produce a wrong number, and they are NOT equally bad: AN INVENTED MEMBER IS INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM A REAL ONE UNTIL SOMEBODY TRIES TO USE IT** — which is the shape of its own family of findings. **THE FOUR INSTANCES: NINE tools for ten, NINE seats for ten, NINE findings for ten, and "the sixth instance" of a family that had SEVEN members** — three counts asserted without an object, and one object chosen for an uncounted number. **AND THE REMEDY IS A PROCEDURE RATHER THAN A PREFERENCE: WHEN A NUMBER AND ITS MEMBERS DISAGREE, COUNT THE MEMBERS AND LET THE NUMBER FALL OUT** — the NUMBER moves, always, **because THE MEMBERS ARE THE EVIDENCE.** **AND THE QUESTION, NOT ONLY THE OBJECT (added 2026-10-06, the fourth member of this rule's family in one shift): A NUMBER WHOSE QUESTION IS UNSTATED IS THE EASIEST WRONG NUMBER TO PUBLISH BY ACCIDENT — `grep` FOR A STRING COUNTS MENTIONS while `grep` FOR AN IMPORT PATH COUNTS IMPORTERS, and the second was sent to the lead as the first.** **So a number travels with its QUESTION as well as its object and unit: NAME THE QUESTION THE NUMBER ANSWERS, or the number will be read as answering whichever question the reader happens to be holding.**
16. A CHECK THAT PASSES WHILE BLIND TO THE THING IT CLAIMS. (Beside rules 11/12/13 — the same act, extending a check past what it measured.) **THE PARENT STATEMENT, of which the instances below are DIMENSIONS rather than siblings: BEFORE TRUSTING A CHECK, ASK WHAT DIMENSION IT CANNOT SEE** — case in a case-sensitive grep, the preview boundary in a body read, the host that answers in a guessed hostname, and **hierarchy in a content search** (the fourth medium, added 2026-10-06: a packet document's H3 label could not scope content sitting under a later H2, and a search built to find stale SENTENCES could not see it). **In every one the instrument could confirm its own sentences and was INCAPABLE OF SEEING THE DIMENSION THE DEFECT LIVED IN** — which is what makes the next medium's version predictable rather than surprising. Five instances from tonight: (i) the CORS preflight, which passes while the actual-response path is the thing under test; (ii) the reviewer's mutation, which failed the BUILD instead of the assertion, so the assertion was never exercised; (iii) the case-sensitive grep for a capitalised test name (rule 11's incident, `57de3715`); (iv) `document.body.textContent`, which glues adjacent elements together so the word-boundary rule never matched; (v) the mission and plan documents read as state (rule 10's incident). **The same act runs in the OPPOSITE direction, and only the scope line tells you which:** an unscoped `gofmt -l .` from `agenthub_go/` prints **57** files — every one under `.gomodcache/` or `.gotmp/`, caches and build temp — so it REPORTS A PROBLEM WHERE NONE EXISTS, while the scoped `gofmt -l` over tracked sources prints zero. A check can fail while blind to nothing real as easily as it can pass while blind to the thing it claims. A green (or red) check names the surface it observed, or it is not a check. **A CLAUSE ON THIS RULE, and it is the family's INVERSE INSTANCE (2026-10-06, from web-dev's dev-socket fix `c69d128a`, 7 paths): A SURFACE REPORTING HEALTHY IS NOT EVIDENCE THAT IT REACHED THE THING IT NAMES — THE CHECK IS A BEHAVIOUR ONLY THE REAL TARGET CAN PRODUCE.** The symptom was INVERTED: the app reported a bare **Offline**, while the measured truth was that its socket reached the **DEV SERVER'S OWN** websocket server, reported **CONNECTED** and showed **Live** against nothing — the dev proxy forwarded `/api` to the backend but had **no `/ws` entry**, so the dev server accepted the upgrade itself. **THE DISCRIMINATOR THAT PROVED IT WAS THE NO-TOKEN CASE:** through the dev port the backend's own close **1008** and its reason text arrived **byte-identical to a direct backend probe** — so **ONE BEHAVIOUR THAT CAN ONLY COME FROM THE REAL SERVICE IS WORTH MORE THAN ANY NUMBER OF SUCCESSFUL OPENS.** **AND THE PROXY WAS DELIBERATELY LEFT UNTESTED, WITH THE REASON STATED:** a test asserting the configuration TEXT would pass whether or not the proxy works, so the evidence is the **WIRE MEASUREMENT** instead — the dropped-half discipline with a different reason for the absence, and the reason is what makes the absence defensible. **AND ITS SECOND HALF, WHICH IS DIAGNOSIS RATHER THAN PREVENTION (2026-10-06; go-dev's rule, passed on by the reviewer with the credit going the direction it costs): WHEN THE INSTRUMENT AND THE OBJECT DISAGREE, SUSPECT THE INSTRUMENT FIRST — AND THE TELL IS THAT THE MISMATCH IS EXPLICABLE IN UNITS THE OBJECT NEVER USED.** The instances are all from tonight, and **each one is a NUMBER IN THE WRONG VOCABULARY:** **a comment run-on that is not a sentence anyone wrote**; **a span comparison of unequal lengths, where the difference lives in an EXTRACTION FILTER rather than in a clause**; and **ELEVEN HUNDRED stderr lines from a formatter, which are ONE LINE PER FILE and not one per failure.** **THE GENERALISATION THAT MAKES IT A RULE RATHER THAN ANECDOTES, in the reviewer's phrasing: THE COUNT IS ALWAYS IN THE INSTRUMENT'S UNITS AND THE ERROR IS ALWAYS IN THE OBJECT'S** — **a grep count, an exit code, a test total and a character length are the INSTRUMENT's arithmetic, while a sentence, a file set and a formatted file are the OBJECT's.** **So the check is a COMPARISON OF UNITS: IF THE DISCREPANCY NEEDS THE INSTRUMENT'S VOCABULARY TO EXPLAIN IT, THE INSTRUMENT IS THE HYPOTHESIS.** **AND IT IS THIS RULE'S SECOND HALF, which is how the reviewer framed it and it is better than a fifth sibling: "MAKE THE INSTRUMENT ABLE TO SEE THE PRESENCE" IS PREVENTION, AND THIS IS DIAGNOSIS** — **and tonight needed BOTH, because FOUR FALSE REPORTS were caught by the diagnosis AFTER the prevention rule had been written.** **The honest shape, recorded because it is the point: THE RULES DID NOT STOP THE FALSE STARTS — THEY MADE THEM CATCHABLE, AND EVERY AUTHOR CAUGHT THEIR OWN.** **AND THE FAMILY'S FIRST PREVENTION INSTANCE — the first time tonight that MEASURING FIRST STOPPED A FALSE FINDING RATHER THAN CORRECTING ONE (2026-10-06; the reviewer, reviewing the mirror-alignment change): it went to CHECK A CLAIM IT DID NOT BELIEVE — the comment said the parser's duplicate-key rule is LAST-WINS while the documentation suggested EXACT-MATCH PREFERENCE — so it PREDICTED A DIVERGENCE, STATED THE PREDICTION, AND MEASURED. GO IS LAST-WINS IN BOTH KEY ORDERS, so the mirror and its comment were BOTH RIGHT and THE HYPOTHESIS WAS WRONG.** **THE GENERALISATION: IT HAD COLLAPSED TWO QUESTIONS THAT SHARE A WORD — the documentation's sentence is about WHICH FIELD A KEY FILLS, the claim is about WHICH OF TWO DUPLICATE KEYS WINS — which is THE SAME CLASS AS THE INSTRUMENT FAILURES ABOVE, ONE LEVEL UP: A TEXT READ AS ANSWERING A QUESTION IT WAS NOT ABOUT.** **So this rule carries BOTH halves in one shape: a TOOL can mislead by measuring in its own units, and a SOURCE can mislead by being read as answering a question it was not about.** **The completeness is recorded too: it LOOKED FOR THE DIVERGENCE IN THE OTHER DIRECTION as well and REPORTED THE SEARCH THAT FOUND NOTHING (so it was NOT ONE-SIDED), and the code change verified clean on TWO OTHER AXES measured by RUNNING THE AUTHORITY rather than reading a description of it.** **AND A SECOND INSTANCE THAT CHANGES A WORD USED ALL NIGHT: "DEAD" HAS TWO MEANINGS AND ONLY THE COMPILER SEES THE DIFFERENCE.** The deletion `9a0b5583` removed the dead flag, the parity test that could not run, and that test's part of a shared fixture; **the substantive finding: A GREP FOR THE NAME RETURNED THE SIGNATURES AND NO CALLERS, SO THE PARAMETER LOOKED UNREFERENCED — while the COMPILER FAILED ON FOUR POSITIONAL CALL SITES that pass the boolean WITHOUT EVER NAMING IT.** **So "dead" was TRUE OF THE VALUE and FALSE OF THE ARGUMENT POSITIONS — A NAME SEARCH CANNOT ESTABLISH THAT AN ARGUMENT IS UNREFERENCED, because POSITIONAL ARGUMENTS CARRY NO NAME TO SEARCH FOR** (the same shape as the rest of this rule: an instrument answering a question it could never see). **AND THE DELETION'S EVIDENCE SHAPE, recorded as the FORM FOR A REMOVAL: the skip is gone BECAUSE THE TEST IS GONE rather than because it is reported differently, PROVED BY COUNTS BEFORE AND AFTER (one fewer result, one fewer skip); the fixture was pruned BY THE KEY THE REMOVED TEST READS, after checking WHICH TEST LOOPS OVER WHICH KEY rather than inferring ownership from the file's name; it went from 400,000 bytes to 4,000 with the TWO KEYS THE SURVIVING TESTS READ untouched; and the BYTECODE files were removed only after BOTH a tracking query AND an ignore query said untracked and ignored — the answer being "untracked and ignored" rather than "looks like build output".** **AND THE HONEST BOUNDARY: the removed fixture section CANNOT BE CHECKED AGAINST ITS SOURCE ANY MORE, because the library it mirrored NO LONGER EXISTS — that section is gone with NO WAY TO RE-DERIVE IT, which is a LEGITIMATE CONSEQUENCE OF THE ARCHIVE rather than an unverified claim.** **The bytecode removals do not appear in the commit, since UNTRACKED FILES ARE NOT STAGED.** **AND ITS READ-INSTRUMENT SIBLING, ADDED 2026-10-06 FROM SEVEN INSTANCES IN ONE EVENING ACROSS FIVE SEATS — A READ TRUNCATED BY THE TOOL THAT CARRIES IT.** **THE FAMILY: (1) the shell tool's echoed output carries an **ANNOUNCED PER-LINE CAP OF 768 CHARACTERS**, which cut a queue-row JSON read mid-string and was caught **only because the parser failed loudly** (`Unterminated string starting at column 747`) — **and the correction, forced by a direct question from the lead, is recorded here because the instance had been OVERSTATED: THE PIPELINE CONTAINED NO `head`/`tail`, SO IT IS NOT the self-bounded root cause, AND THE ANNOUNCEMENT (*"Some lines truncated to 768 chars"*) WAS PRESENT AND WAS READ AS DISPLAY SCENERY; the repair was to redirect the read to a file, after which the same field returned with ALL 24 MARKERS AND NO BOUNDARY AT ALL. The "clean boundary at exactly 2,300" quoted from this instance was a HYPOTHETICAL, stated in the past tense and read as an observation — WITHDRAWN, because the difference decides whether a self-inflicted bound or an unannounced cap is to blame, and only the first is true here. The lesson that survives is a different one, and it is the harder half of this rule: the announcement WAS made, and a reader dismissed it**; (2) a grep that matched the pattern it was searching for; (3) a bounded preview (`rig queue show`) mistaken for the store; (4) a search for a LABEL instead of the content (a verb rendered, but its name appeared nowhere); (5) a test's CASE NAMES read instead of the ASSERTION placed after them, so NOT VERIFIED was written about something verified; (6) a database renderer that truncates a cell at ~40 characters in table form and ~90 in single-row form — THE TABLE LOOKS COMPLETE BECAUSE EVERY CELL ENDS AT THE SAME PLACE; and (7) A GREP THAT READS ONLY THE FIRST 4 MB OF A LARGE FILE, the worst of the seven because IT PRESENTS AN ABSENCE — the reading a person is most likely to act on.** **THE RULE: BEFORE YOU ACT ON A READING, ASK WHICH INSTRUMENT PRODUCED IT AND WHERE THAT INSTRUMENT'S BOUND IS. AND: THE TRUNCATION THAT IS ANNOUNCED IS THE ONE YOU ALREADY CAUGHT — HUNT THE UNANNOUNCED CAP.** **AND A CORRECTION TO THAT LAST SENTENCE FROM THIS FAMILY'S OWN FIRST INSTANCE: AN ANNOUNCEMENT IS ONLY AN ADVANTAGE IF IT IS READ AS DATA RATHER THAN AS SCENERY — that instance's truncation WAS announced (*"Some lines truncated to 768 chars"*) and was still acted past, so the announcement buys nothing on its own; only the parser, failing loudly, caught it. The companion line below, "not one of the seven was caused by carelessness", is amended by the same instance, because dismissing an announcement is exactly what carelessness looks like from the inside — and the useful form of the rule is therefore not "trust the announcement" but READ THE BOUND AS A VALUE, IN THE SAME BREATH AS THE READ.** **AND THE MEMBER THAT EXPLAINS WHY THAT INSTANCE TRAVELLED TO EIGHT SEATS: A HYPOTHETICAL AND AN OBSERVATION ARE INDISTINGUISHABLE ONCE THEY ARE PASTED INTO A SUMMARY — the author writes the modal ("would have read exactly like a clean boundary at 2,300") and THE PASTE STRIPS IT, so the reading arrives looking measured and is repeated by everyone downstream, including the seat that never saw the original.** **The culprit is therefore not the author but THE SUMMARY AS AN INSTRUMENT, which is the same shape as the echo and the preview one level up — so a summary that carries a reading MUST CARRY ITS STATUS (measured / hypothesised / relayed), and a reader who receives one without a status is holding a claim with no instrument attached.** **THE WORKS, FINAL — AND THERE ARE TWO DIFFERENT TRUNCATIONS WITH DIFFERENT REMEDIES: (a) A PER-LINE CAP OF 768 CHARACTERS, announced as *"Some lines truncated to 768 chars"* WITH NO ARTIFACT POINTER, AND THE ARTIFACT DOES NOT FIX IT — `:raw` DOES** (verified: an 821-character single line arrived whole under `:raw` with its end marker intact, and the same file without `:raw` lost the marker at 768); **(b) A LINE-RANGE ELISION, announced as *"Read artifact://N for full output"* — THAT one the artifact fixes, read RAW (`:raw:N-M` selects chunks of the store) — AND PAGE IT, because `:raw` DOES NOT LIFT THE 300-LINE CAP: on a 41,961-byte artifact, `:raw` removes the anchors and breaks the self-pointing loop AS PUBLISHED and STILL SHOWS LINES 1-300 OF 763, announced with the total and the next step. SO COMPLETENESS NEEDS PAGING (`:301`, `:601`) OR A RANGE, and `:raw:-N` is the one-call way to the tail. AND THAT CAP IS HARMLESS FOR THE REASON THE WHOLE RULE RESTS ON: ITS ANNOUNCEMENT STATES THE TOTAL AND THE NEXT STEP, SO IT SITS OUTSIDE THE LIMIT IT DESCRIBES — same file, same reader as the pointer that hurt, and the only difference is whether THE REMEDY AND THE ANNOUNCEMENT ARE THE SAME OBJECT.** **AND FOR THE `xd://` DEVICE WRITES — `manage_task`, `manage_context`, `submit_feedback` — THERE IS NO REDIRECT AT ALL, SO NEVER ACT ON A LONG FIELD FROM THE ECHO: RE-DERIVE IT FROM THE FILE OR THE REPO, because the write's echo is the only copy you will see and it is a preview.** **AND THE WORST CASE IS A JSON DUMP OF A LONG FIELD, because it is ONE LONG LINE BY CONSTRUCTION — which is the shape of every structured read we make.** **So: REDIRECT TO A FILE AND PARSE THE FILE; `:raw` FOR A LONG SINGLE LINE; THE ARTIFACT URI FOR AN ELIDED RANGE, read raw; NEVER PIPE A STRUCTURED READ THROUGH `head`/`tail` IF YOU WILL ACT ON A VALUE FROM IT; MAKE THE ARITHMETIC CLOSE ON LIST READS with a counter that cannot undercount (`grep -c` with an empty pattern — NOT `wc -l`, which undercounts by one when the last line has no trailing newline) **and state how many instruments agreed**, since one number measured three ways is not three readings; `--full` on the queue, because the default `show` is a bounded preview by its own help text; READ A TEST'S ASSERTION, not its case names; READ ONE FIELD FROM THE PARSED OBJECT, never from a grep over echoed text; WHERE A READ IS LONG ENOUGH TO SPILL OR BE TRUNCATED, SAY WHICH INSTRUMENT YOU READ IT WITH, because a number that arrives without its instrument cannot be audited downstream; and FOR A DID-THIS-LAND QUESTION USE THE COMMIT FILE LIST, never the subject line.** **AND THE DEFINITION THAT EXPLAINS WHY ANY OF THIS MATTERS: THE DISTINGUISHING QUESTION IS NOT "IS THERE A CAP" BUT WHO TOLD YOU ABOUT IT — THE BOUNDS THAT HURT ARE THE ONES WHERE THE ANNOUNCEMENT AND THE REMEDY ARE THE SAME OBJECT** (the announced shell truncation is harmless because its announcement sits OUTSIDE the thing it limits; the artifact pointer is dangerous because the announcement IS the limit). **AND A THIRD SPECIES: A BOUND THAT IS WRONG RATHER THAN UNMARKED — the instrument disagreeing with its own documentation (a verb refusing `limit=10` while its schema documents `limit` as a number).** **AND THE ROOT CAUSE OF THE WHOLE FAMILY, MEASURED WITH ONE COMMAND RUN TWICE: NEVER BOUND A STRUCTURED READ INTO THE ECHO.** The raw `rig queue transitions <id>` **PRINTS THE ANNOUNCEMENT** (*"[Some lines truncated to 768 bytes. Read artifact://191 for full output]"*) and the artifact holds **all 41,961 bytes**; **the SAME COMMAND `| head -c 2000` CUTS A TOKEN IN HALF AND ANNOUNCES NOTHING.** **THE MECHANISM: THE SPILL ONLY FIRES WHEN THE ECHO WOULD BE TOO LARGE, SO BOUNDING YOUR OWN OUTPUT MAKES THE ECHO SMALL ENOUGH THAT THE HARNESS NEVER SPILLS AND NEVER TELLS YOU — YOUR OWN BOUND REMOVES THE SAFETY NET AND TURNS AN ANNOUNCED TRUNCATION INTO A SILENT ONE.** **We all bound by habit to keep echoes readable, and THE HABIT IS WHAT DISABLES THE DETECTOR — which is why six seats hit this in one evening and none of them saw it.** **So: BOUND INTO A FILE, NOT INTO THE ECHO (`cmd > file`, then parse or read the file); READ THE ARTIFACT THE HARNESS NAMES, WITH `:raw`; and NEVER PIPE A STRUCTURED READ THROUGH `head` OR `tail` IF YOU INTEND TO ACT ON A VALUE OUT OF IT — if you must bound for readability, do it ONLY AFTER you have parsed what you need.** **AND THE MODEL, which is the third appearance of one shape in one evening: THE ECHO IS A PREVIEW AND THE ARTIFACT IS THE STORE — the same as the queue's bounded `show` versus `--full`, and the same as a commit subject versus its file list.** **AND NOT ONE OF THE SEVEN WAS CAUSED BY CARELESSNESS, which is the point: an instrument with an unmarked bound hands a careful reader a confident wrong answer.**
**AND THE EIGHTH INSTANCE IS A DIFFERENT MEMBER OF THE FAMILY — AN INDICATOR MISTAKEN FOR THE OUTCOME, the same thing as 'I wrote the audit result before running it' and 'I repeated an earlier observation instead of re-measuring': THE SIGNAL ARRIVED, THE THING HAD NOT.** **Instance: an edit tool reported success while its replacement had CLIPPED the opening line of the item it anchored on — caught by reading the file back rather than by trusting the success, and it is why the checklist is intact.** **SO: A TOOL'S SUCCESS REPORT IS AN INDICATOR, NOT A RESULT — READ THE ARTEFACT BACK.**
17. A FIX IS NOT VERIFIED ON A SURFACE THAT CANNOT OBSERVE IT. (Beside rule 16.) Under `AUTH_ENABLED=false` the REST half accepts any bearer AND skips the CORS middleware, so three of tonight's findings can only show at the SOCKET, in the APP SESSION handling, or in the RESPONSE HEADER PAIR — and the report must name WHICH SURFACE was driven per fix. Companion instance, the CORS-preflight shape one layer out: a server still running WITH the variable set would let the seat-read fix pass for the WRONG reason, so it is restarted WITHOUT it first.

18. WHEN A SHARED FILE CARRIES ANOTHER SEAT'S UNCOMMITTED CHANGE, DO NOT NAME THAT FILE IN YOUR COMMIT — and there is nothing to stage first: write your own entry and commit it immediately, by pathspec, so the window never exists. The pathspec commit stops a seat from taking the WHOLE INDEX; it does NOT stop a seat from taking a SHARED FILE that happens to carry a sibling's pending line. Incidents, the same sweep in both directions: `b0a1f510` (the earlier one) and `0565da9f` — a FRONTEND commit staged `TEST-CHANGELOG.md` wholesale while go-dev2's bridge machine-token test entry was uncommitted in the working tree, so that entry rode a commit by another seat: content intact, ATTRIBUTION wrong. Changelogs are the shared files in practice, so the immediate-commit habit (write the entry, commit it, then move on) is now the expected one. **Companion shape from the same reviewer verdict — check, do not assume:** rather than presume `O_TRUNC` would overwrite, the reviewer read the env-file writer; `write_env_file` (`scripts/openrig_bridge.py:357-377`) filters only its OWN two keys (`kept = [line for line in lines if line.split("=", 1)[0].strip() not in values]`), so every non-owned key survives, and it `chmod`s 0600 AFTER writing because "O_CREAT's mode is masked by the umask" (its own docstring). **THE STRONGER FORM OF THE SAME REMEDY, as a clause (2026-10-06; **the REMEDY is this seat's** — two in-flight rules staged in a scratch file OUTSIDE the tree, removed once it had done its job — and go-dev2's contribution was the ARTICULATION, which they flagged themselves rather than taking the credit): WHEN TWO SEATS CONTEND FOR ONE PATH, MOVE THE WORK OUT OF THE CONTENDED OBJECT RATHER THAN TAKING TURNS ON IT** — it REMOVES the window instead of managing it, which is the same reason SEQUENCING beat careful staging and the same reason a push BY HASH is safe under a freeze. The instance: two in-flight rules were staged in a scratch file OUTSIDE the tree so the other seat's gate could pass, and the scratch file was REMOVED once it had done its job. **A CLAUSE ON THIS RULE, from the same shared index a minute later (2026-10-06, this seat): THE REFUSAL IS LOUD AND THE RIDE-THROUGH IT MIGHT HAVE HIDDEN IS SILENT — SO THE ERROR MESSAGE IS EXACTLY THE PART OF THE EVENT A READER SHOULD NOT ACT ON.** `git commit` was refused with `Unable to create '.git/index.lock': File exists` while a sibling committed **in the same instant**; its commit landed on top of mine, and **the question that matters is whether MY STAGED CHANGES RODE IT.** The answer came from **MEASURING THE TREE rather than reading the error**: a case-sensitive grep of `HEAD` for each of the three additions returned **0**, and `git diff --stat HEAD` showed the six changed lines still **uncommitted** — so **nothing rode**, and the re-commit landed them. **The same shape as every other check tonight: the instrument that REPORTS the failure is not the instrument that can SEE THE CONSEQUENCE.** **AND A CLAUSE ON CITATIONS, learned in the same minute: A HASH IN A MESSAGE IS A VINTAGE CLAIM** — a bare hex string names an object **without saying what it is or when it was true**, so a reader either guesses or re-derives. A message reported `59510ad3 is approved` beside the G4 line; that hash turned out to be **another seat's BRIDGE commit** (`fix(bridge): name the cloud's verdict…`) rather than the G4 line (`106befcb`, `c41118ab`), so the two were **recorded apart rather than merged** — **a citation earns its hash only by carrying its subject beside it.** **AND IT ESCAPES IN THE OTHER DIRECTION TOO (2026-10-06, the SAME seat twenty minutes later — the second instance of rule 30's shape tonight): NOT ONLY CAN A NUMBER BE WRITTEN BEFORE IT IS MEASURED, A TEMPLATE CAN BE SENT WITHOUT BEING FILLED** — and **both leave a sentence that reads as COMPLETE.** The instance: a report carried a literal `$H` where the commit hash belonged (`committed $H`, `$H is HEAD`) because the value sat inside a **quoted-heredoc template that never expanded it**, so **the claim arrived WITHOUT A USABLE IDENTITY and the reader had to re-derive it** — exactly what this clause forbids. **THE CHEAP CHECK IS THE SAME FOR BOTH DIRECTIONS: READ THE SENTENCE AS THE READER WILL — if it contains a value, confirm it IS a value and not a NAME FOR ONE.** **AND THE STRONGER CHECK, from a THIRD slip in the same stretch (2026-10-06, this seat): READ WHAT THE HASH NAMES, NOT JUST THAT IT IS A HASH — CONFIRM THE HASH NAMES THE THING THE SENTENCE SAYS IT NAMES**, because **a wrong hash is indistinguishable from a right one to anyone who does not look up its subject.** **THE THREE SLIPS IN ONE LINE, because the pattern is specific: ALL THREE WERE THE SAME FAILURE IN THREE COSTUMES — a NUMBER BEFORE ITS MEASUREMENT, a TEMPLATE SENT UNFILLED, and a CORRECT HASH ATTACHED TO THE WRONG SUBJECT — and what they share is that EACH SENTENCE LOOKED COMPLETE. That is this family's whole thesis, and this seat's own sequence is now its evidence.**
19. AN UNREACHABILITY CLAIM MUST NAME THE SCOPE IT WAS ESTABLISHED OVER. "Unreachable" is true of the callers that were read, and its own words are the boundary — the same shape as a check naming the surface it can observe (rule 17). The rule is NOT that unreachable branches are dangerous: it is that the SAME branch is LOUD or SILENT depending on who arrives, and the scope line is what tells you which. Incident: go-dev reported the startup-failure fallback unreachable because every seat-rendering path can derive the origin, and the reviewer verified the THREE call sites (`seat_mount.go:193` and `seat_rigspec_mount.go:111` pass the request; `call_seat_wiring.go:29` passes `nil` with a context `mcp_routes.go:296` stashes the origin into) — so the claim holds FOR THESE THREE; a FOURTH caller that renders a seat with no request, no context and no variable would reach the SAME branch and get a SILENCE, where the third caller's empty return is a LOUD error.

20. A CLEANUP LIST ENTRY IS A CLAIM LIKE ANY OTHER AND SHOULD BE MEASURED BEFORE IT IS WORKED OR REPEATED. (Beside rule 14 — the same rule from a third direction.) A plan document, a recipe and a cleanup list are all prose claims about work, and the only thing that turns one into a fact is running it. Incident: the mission's cleanup list names a `gofmt` finding in the subtask controller; measured 2026-10-06 with the tool itself, `gofmt -l` prints ZERO over every tracked `.go` file, over `cmd`+`fastmcp`, and over `fastmcp/task_management/interface`, and `gofmt -d` on the named controller has no diff — the finding is not on this tree, so the entry was never work (rule 14, one layer out: a claim nobody had run).

21. THE TESTED PATH AND THE WIRED PATH ARE TWO DIFFERENT OBJECTS, AND NO AMOUNT OF CARE INSIDE THE TESTED ONE CLOSES THE GAP BETWEEN THEM. (The third of one family: rule 16 is a check blind to what it claims, rule 17 is a check on a surface that cannot observe it, and this is a check ON A DIFFERENT OBJECT THAN THE ONE THAT SHIPS.) The class cannot be caught from inside the tested path — that is why it survives a green suite — so it needs one exercise of the WIRED path: a request through the running server, the real scan over real input. Evidence, all from tonight and all independent: the CORS defect survived a CORRECT preflight test (the preflight half is genuinely pinned; the actual-response path is not the object that test measured); the four naming variants were invisible to a phrasing grep; a fake `500` asserted in a route test read as coverage for a route whose real path never produced one; and the seatcheck audit row proved DETECTION only when the scan ran over an input carrying BOTH a forged and a legitimate line (verified in the `7e576758` tests). The reviewer demanding the curl pair through a running server, rather than accepting the green suite, is this rule in practice.

22. A GREEN ON A SUBSET IS NOT A GREEN ON THE PACKAGE. (Beside rules 16, 17 and 21 — the same family: a check that measured something NARROWER than what it claims.) Incident: go-dev2 moved an expectation in the HTTP surface for the occupant blank-keeps ruling and MISSED a third pin of the old behaviour in `manage_seat_controller_test.go` (`fastmcp/seat_management/interface/mcp_controllers/`), because the same service is also called from the MCP controller — so `mcp_controllers` was RED from that commit while the author's report said the change was swept clean. The package only LOOKED green because it ran the occupant cases BY NAME rather than the TREE; it surfaced the moment the whole `seat_management` tree ran for the next row. Both halves of the recovery belong with the rule: (a) run the PACKAGE or the TREE before claiming a sweep, and (b) when a missed instance is suspected, GREP FOR THE SHAPE rather than the file — the sweep grepped the repo for any remaining pin of the old expectation and found none. The same distinction one level down: the seeder fix rests on `GetVersion` returning nil for an absent row, which is a contract READ (documented at `module_repository.go:98` and `seat_type_repository.go:169`) rather than RUN in that environment, and whether it needs a real database is the open question.

23. THE CONTRACT EXISTED ON BOTH SIDES AND ONLY ONE OF THEM IMPLEMENTED IT. (Beside rule 21, the same family; the distinction between them: rule 21 is a check on a DIFFERENT OBJECT than the one that ships, and this is a CONTRACT WITH TWO SIDES WHERE ONLY ONE END IS CHECKED — the test that proves the client handles `1008` passes whether or not any server ever sends `1008`.) Incident: `WebSocketClient.ts:306-307` already mapped close code `1008` to `authenticationFailed(event.reason)`, while the server answered a bare `403` BEFORE the upgrade — which a browser turns into `1006` with NO reason — so the client was written to consume a message the server never sent, and NOTHING was red because each half was self-consistent. Same shape as the two naming variants, the CORS wildcard that survived a correct preflight, and the two presence-dependent op lists: a rule written down in one place and applied in another. The recovery is the same in both directions: FIND THE OTHER SIDE and make the WIRE the object under test. (Fixed in `b6d5db94`: one auth decision for REST and the socket, and a rejected upgrade is now completed and closed with `1008` plus a reason the client can read.) **AND A PROPERTY FROM THE SAME REVIEW ROUND, recorded as a SHAPE rather than as a bug report (2026-10-06; the reviewer's packet-3 verdicts): A MIRROR OF AN AUTHORITY CAN DRIFT IN EITHER DIRECTION, AND THE STRICTER DIRECTION IS A FALSE REFUSAL.** The instance: **the frontend's block validator mirrors Go's parser and REJECTS an explicit JSON `null` that Go treats as ABSENT**, plus **CASE-SENSITIVE field names where Go is CASE-INSENSITIVE** — so **a block the RENDERER accepts cannot be SUBMITTED from the form.** **AND THE FIX DIRECTION IS THE RULE: ALIGN THE MIRROR TO THE AUTHORITY** — **the parser is the source of truth for what a block IS, so keep only the refusals the authority itself makes.** **Both differences were found by RUNNING THE GO PARSER AGAINST THE FRONTEND'S RULE rather than by reading the diff**, which is what made them visible.

24. GATED TESTS THAT SKIP ARE NOT COVERED BY YOUR GREEN. (Beside rule 22 — the same act one layer down, and SHARPER because the SKIP IS SILENT: rule 22 is about running a SUBSET, this is about running a subset THAT SILENTLY EXCLUDES ITSELF.) Incident: go-dev2's "all green" reports for the seat model ran with NO DATABASE, so the DB-gated integration tests (the `SEAT_TEST_DATABASE_URL`-gated ones, e.g. `TestSeatRepositoriesIntegration`) SKIPPED, and a green on the ungated tree read as a green on the whole suite. Recovery, the same shape as the others: NAME the tests that skipped, or run the tree with the gate satisfied — a skip is a result to report, not an absence to omit.

25. A RUN THAT EXECUTES ZERO TESTS PRINTS A SUCCESSFUL TAIL. (Beside rules 16, 22 and 24 — the common form is **THE COUNT IS THE EVIDENCE, NOT THE LAST LINE**: in all three the command exited 0 while doing LESS than the reader assumed.) Incident: go-dev's first attempt at verifying its new token test printed "no tests to run" for the sibling packages — a filtered run matching NOTHING — and a reader of the tail would have called that green; it caught it by re-running the package properly rather than accepting the output. Recovery: run the package or the file with a COUNT YOU CAN SEE, and treat a ZERO COUNT as a failure of the CHECK rather than a pass of the code.

26. A CLAIM PUT IN A MESSAGE BEFORE IT IS MEASURED IS A CLAIM LIKE ANY OTHER. (Rule 10 and rule 20 one layer out — it does not matter that the claim arrived as a message between seats rather than as a plan, a recipe or a cleanup list; THE MEDIUM IS NOT THE WARRANT.) The recovery is the same as always: run the check, and REPORT THE CORRECTION AGAINST YOURSELF. Incident, a self-retraction: the reviewer had reported that `.gitignore:67` (`*secret*`) hides the whole `secretscan/` directory, so the package and its testdata would never be committed — then measured itself wrong: `git ls-files` lists three tracked files under it, and `.gitignore:71` is a deliberate negation keeping the directory out of `*secret*`'s reach. Pairs with the reviewer's own relay-not-claim sentence from the same hour: what arrives relayed still has to be verified against the artefact.

27. IN A SHARED WORKING TREE, AN UNCOMMITTED EDIT IS NOT DURABLE — AND THIS FAILURE IS SILENT. (Measured 2026-10-06 by web-dev; sharpened here in go-dev's terms.) Every OTHER shared-resource rule we wrote tonight FAILS LOUDLY OR LEAVES A TRACE — the index, the build, the socket — while THIS ONE REVERTS SILENTLY, and its only symptom is an ANCHOR THAT NO LONGER MATCHES, which reads as the editor's own mistake. The failure is in the TOOLING used to commit: the pre-commit hook STASHES unstaged files and RESTORES them (visible in every commit of this session as `Stashing unstaged files to …` / `Restored changes from …`), and every seat shares ONE working tree — so another seat's commit can revert your uncommitted file to HEAD with NO error to either side; web-dev lost in-flight `AuthContext.tsx` edits and noticed only because its next edit's anchor no longer matched. It REMOVES work rather than corrupting it, and the natural response — redo the edit — hides the loss completely, which is why this belongs with the INVISIBLE-FAILURE family (rules 13/16), not with the attribution rules. Recovery, all three parts: (a) **COMMIT AS SOON AS A CHANGE IS VERIFIED AND ITS PATHSPEC IS EXACT** — an uncommitted edit is NOT durable here; (b) **RE-READ THE ANCHOR BEFORE EACH EDIT, and ASSUME THE HAZARD rather than your memory when it does not match — but do NOT blindly re-apply**, because a blind re-apply on top of a PARTIALLY reverted file turns a silent revert into a CORRUPTED FILE; (c) **IF YOU FIND A FILE REVERTED, SAY SO** rather than redoing it quietly, since a quiet redo hides a lost change underneath. **FOUR DIRECTIONS IN ONE FAMILY — TWO FALSE NEGATIVES AND TWO FALSE POSITIVES, ONE AUTHORITATIVE ANSWER (2026-10-06; (ii) from the reviewer's disclosure, which named the window and left the uncertainty standing, (iii) from web-dev's own correction after mis-diagnosing itself, and (iv) from go-dev2 during the freeze window):** (i) pre-commit stashes unstaged files across another seat's commit, and a FAILED restore loses them PERMANENTLY; (ii) **ANY COMMIT-LEVEL RESTORE USED AS AN UNDO** — `git checkout -- <path>` — reaches anyone uncommitted under that path and loses them PERMANENTLY (which is how the reviewer probably lost web-dev's in-flight edit while undoing its own mutation); (iii) **A READ INSIDE A HOOK WINDOW READS FILES AT HEAD**, because the hook has them stashed while it runs — so a marker grep returns NOTHING and looks EXACTLY like a wipe, while NOTHING WAS LOST. **(iii-b, added 2026-10-06 from the lead's warning and go-dev's measurement of it — the same clause with an instrument that is not a grep: A TEST OR A BUILD RUN IN THAT WINDOW READS THE OLD CODE, so a suite can hand back a result about a file that no longer says what its author wrote — go-dev lost real time twice to exactly that, a test answering `Unknown error occurred` because its uncommitted fix was stashed at the moment it ran. The reader's rule is unchanged and sharper here: DO NOT TRUST A CHECK WHOSE INPUT WAS THE SHARED TREE MID-COMMIT; re-run it after the window, and if you must have a reading during one, take it from a path you control.)** (iv) **A STALE INDEX READ** shows a CLEAN file as modified — a lone `M` in `git status` with an EMPTY `git diff` and an EMPTY `git diff --cached` — and looks like work THAT WAS NOT DONE; it appeared right after a checkout inside a hook window, the file was in fact committed and clean, and it CLEARED once the index refreshed. All four produce a symptom that reads as a fact about the tree, and the discriminator is whether you **WROTE or only READ** during the window: (i) and (ii) are LOSSES, (iii) and (iv) are FALSE READINGS — one a false negative, one a false positive. **THE STRUCTURAL POINT, which is why this belongs with rule 16 rather than as a footnote here:** IN EVERY ONE OF THE FOUR THE INSTRUMENT WAS WRONG AND THE FINDING WAS CORRECT, and in three of them the wrong reading would have produced a wrong **ACTION** — re-doing work that exists, or reporting a loss that did not happen. **The reader's rule (go-dev2's): BEFORE CONCLUDING ANYTHING FROM `git status`, CONFIRM WITH `git diff` AND `git diff --cached`, and treat a lone `M` with an empty diff as a STALE READ.** **And its companion, which extends copy-first from the WRITER to the READER (2026-10-06, the reviewer's third-cause confirmation): TAKE THE READING FROM A PATH YOU CONTROL RATHER THAN FROM THE SHARED TREE MID-COMMIT.** The reviewer watched `resolver_test.go` and `NEXT_GEN.md` each appear modified in one command and unchanged in the next, SECONDS apart, and concluded at the time that a seat was mid-edit and "the tree is a moment rather than a fact"; the better explanation for at least one of those is that **A FILE AT HEAD FOR THE DURATION OF A HOOK IS NOT A STATE A SEAT PASSED THROUGH — IT IS A STATE THE WINDOW PRESENTED**, which makes the hook window a cause distinct from both the stash-across-a-commit and the commit-level restore, producing the symptom with nothing lost and nobody's work touched. Together the two reader rules close the family: the symptom has **FOUR CAUSES AND TWO SIGNS**, `git status` ALONE CANNOT DISTINGUISH ANY OF THEM, the diffs settle what changed, and a reading taken from your own copy settles it without depending on the window at all. That distinction is what makes the recovery usable — as first written it told a reader to re-read before trusting, and a reader who re-reads DURING a hook window gets the false negative and concludes the work is gone; so **re-read AFTER the window, or confirm with `git log` / `git stash list` rather than a marker grep alone**, and the three-part recovery above applies to the two LOSS mechanisms. **The rule for those two: COPY THE FILE OUT FIRST, MUTATE, RESTORE FROM THE COPY, THEN VERIFY** — so the tool that undoes your change cannot reach anyone else's. **The hook-abort variant, hit live by this seat (2026-10-06):** when a pre-commit hook FAILS it has ALREADY MODIFIED files, so the tree is left CHANGED even though the commit did not happen — which is why "re-read before trusting" covers that case too; and **`git log -1` IS NOT A STATEMENT ABOUT YOUR COMMIT while another seat is committing in the same window** (another seat's commit, `2cafa873`, landed as HEAD between two aborted attempts, so `git log -1` showed THEIRS while ours did not yet exist). **AND ITS PREDICTABLE VARIANT, paid for TWICE in one shift (2026-10-06, this seat): A GENERATED FILE WITH NO TRAILING NEWLINE PLUS THE `end-of-file-fixer` MAKES THE FIRST COMMIT ATTEMPT FAIL BY CONSTRUCTION.** **The only pre-commit config in this tree is `agenthub_main/.pre-commit-config.yaml`, and its whitespace and EOF hooks carry NO `files:` scope — only `exclude: ^agenthub_go/` (`:26-29`) — so they fire on `ai_docs/` even though the ruff hooks beside them are scoped to `^agenthub_main/`; the docs index generator (`.claude/hooks/utils/docs_indexer.py`) writes `index.json` WITHOUT a final newline, the hook adds one, the hook exits 1, and the retry lands because the hook's own fix persisted in the worktree.** **THE PRACTICAL SHAPE, so the failure is not misread as one's own mistake: WHEN YOU REGENERATE A GENERATED FILE, EXPECT THE FIRST COMMIT ATTEMPT TO FAIL — re-run the commit and the second attempt passes, because the fix is already in the file.** One operational rule changed with it: **VERIFIED NOW MEANS COMMITTED** — the batch habit stays for RECORDS, but code commits go in as soon as the gates pass. **(v) AND A NEW DIRECTION IN THE SAME FAMILY — A POSITIONAL ANCHOR IN A HOT SECTION FAILS SILENTLY WHEN ANOTHER SEAT APPENDS ABOVE IT (2026-10-06, this seat).** **What happened: an edit anchored on `### Changed` plus the first words of the entry that sat directly beneath it — and go-dev2 had inserted a DDL-defaults entry in exactly that position minutes earlier — matched ACROSS the inserted entry and REPLACED the whole span, so ANOTHER SEAT'S CHANGELOG ENTRY WAS DELETED while the editor's own addition and its neighbour both survived.** **The recovery, recorded because a silent loss deserves the full shape: the block was restored VERBATIM from the parent commit and verified BYTE-IDENTICAL (2,446 bytes) before the repair was committed.** **THE PRACTICE: IN A SECTION SEVERAL SEATS APPEND TO, ANCHOR ON TEXT UNIQUE TO THE ENTRY YOU ARE PLACING BESIDE — NEVER ON ITS POSITION UNDER A SHARED HEADING — and after editing such a section, VERIFY WITH A COUNT OR A DIFF AGAINST THE PARENT rather than by re-reading your own addition, because re-reading your addition cannot see what your edit removed.**

28. A SEARCH THAT LOOKS AT THE WRONG FIELD MISSES ITS OWN SUBJECT. (Beside rule 16 — this is the INSTRUMENT, not the finding: a check blind to the thing it claims to search, and the recovery is the same positive form.) THIRD instance of the field-versus-content mistake in one night, and the cleanest: the reviewer searched its own transcript for the mutation and found NOTHING, because the edit travelled as an edit tool call carrying the path in `args.path` while the filter looked at `args.command` — the search could not match its own subject. The other two, both named by the reviewer: the queue note, and the note-versus-message count (a note table answers "whose row carried a hash", the messages answer "what was concluded"). In all three the instrument was wrong, not the world; the recovery is to search the field the artefact ACTUALLY uses, and to read "no match" as a claim about the pattern AND the field together (rule 15, one layer down).

29. A TEST THAT PINS THE WRONG BEHAVIOUR MUST BE DELETED, WHILE A TEST THAT PINS A SUPERSEDED CONTRACT SHOULD BE UPDATED. (The test-hygiene rule that resolves the apparent tension; the discriminator is ONE question: **WAS THE OLD ASSERTION EVER TRUE OF THE DESIGN?**) The two incidents are the two sides of one rule: (i) the CORS test — `TestWithCORSSimpleRequestDefaultWildcardWithoutCookie` pinned a wildcard beside `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials` under a name that made the BUG read as the safe path; it was never true of the design, so `ff5f10d6` DELETED it and replaced it with `TestWithCORSSimpleRequestDefaultWildcardEchoesOrigin` and `TestWithCORSSimpleRequestDisallowedOrigin`, because any version of it re-asserts the wrong behaviour.
